The agent already carries a fully worked rule — step 9's *Never disclose an unfixed vulnerability on a public tracker*, with a `gh repo view --json isPrivate` visibility check, a prohibition on filing anything that names an exploitable weakness or a missing authn / authz / signature / validation control, routing to a private security channel, and a withhold-and-escalate fallback. Step 4 referenced none of it, and step 4 is the **larger** disclosure: step 9 files one out-of-scope finding, step 4 publishes the entire pre-implementation analysis and remediation plan to the tracker. Issue #212's checklist format sharpened the exposure rather than softening it — an unchecked `- [ ] [Critical] …` item is a precise, machine-readable statement that a named control is missing at a named place, before any fix exists, which is exactly what a reader of a public repository would want. Step 4 now runs the same visibility check and, on a public tracker, takes one of three named routes with the chosen route recorded in the handoff: **phrase every item as the invariant that must hold** rather than the gap that exists (the GFM syntax and severity prefix stay verbatim, so `hephaestus` reads the checklist unchanged) — explicitly **not** sufficient on its own for a `Critical`, because an unchecked Critical still marks where the exploitable gap is whatever the wording; **route the plan to the project's private security channel** (private vulnerability reporting / a draft advisory) with only a neutral pointer issue published, naming the private location so `hephaestus` knows where to read the checklist; or **publish in full because the project owner explicitly accepted that exposure**, quoting where they made that decision, which is L3 and never the agent's to make. When no route is available the plan is withheld and carried in the handoff, the same fallback step 9 already uses. `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php` pins the subordination to step 9, the checklist-sharpens-it reasoning, all three routes, the Critical carve-out on the cheapest one, and the withhold fallback.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: a second run on the same source no longer overwrites a live peer's brief or truncates its audit trail (#221). `agents/daedalus.md` step 2 has all the machinery needed to detect this — a `## PID` field at a fixed position, a format-validated anchor, and a confirmed-dead `kill -0` probe that distinguishes ESRCH from EPERM — and applies it to every brief on disk **except one**: *Stale briefs* deliberately skips "the file this run is about to write". That exclusion is right for a sweep, which deletes; you do not delete a file you are about to write. But the very next bullet then `mv`s this run's own brief onto that same path, so the one file the sweep never probes is precisely the one that gets overwritten, and *Audit trail ledger*'s "Create it empty" resets the peer's accumulated trail alongside it. The collision is not theoretical: *Concurrency & the working-tree write-lock* explicitly lets any number of analysis-only runs overlap with each other and with one writing run, and an analysis-only run takes no write-lock — so nothing serialises `resolve #200` against `analyse #200`, and both derive the identical slug and therefore the identical path. The fix reuses the existing gate rather than inventing a second one: before the `mv`, an already-present `.claude/run/<source-slug>.md` has its `## PID` read with the same fixed-position anchor and probed with the same confirmed-dead gate. A confirmed-dead, format-valid PID means the writing run is gone and the path is free. **Everything else — a successful probe, an EPERM probe, a missing or malformed `## PID`, a token failing the format check — is a live peer**, and the run stops with `Blocked: a live run holds the brief for this slug` instead of overwriting anything. That is the same fail-safe default the sweep, the write-lock reclaim, and *Run cleanup*'s ownership gate already apply: *not mine* is not the same fact as *not alive*. The verdict is ordered rather than binary, because the obvious two-way version blocks a run against itself: a PID **equal to this instance's own `$PPID`** is this run's own brief from an earlier pass through the step, so it is checked **first** and treated as a re-entrant no-op — this run's own PID is by definition alive and would otherwise read as a live peer. The two ledgers now follow the brief's verdict rather than their own — neither `.dispatches` nor `.audit` is created until the probe clears the path, because an audit trail a concurrent run can silently reset is not an audit trail. The remaining gap is declared where the file declares its others: the probe and the `mv` are two steps, so two runs that both probe an absent brief can still both proceed. That window is microseconds against a collision window that was previously the whole run — the same trade-off the claim contract already accepts between its re-read and its apply — and closing it fully would need an exclusive create that the atomic-`mv` requirement rules out, since the `## PID` line must land in the same operation as the file. `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php` pins the uncovered-file reasoning, the reused anchor and probe, the Czech `Blocked` wording, the fail-safe default, and the ledger ordering.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: `daedalus` validates the source-slug before it reaches a path or a deletion command (#220). `agents/athena.md` *Review worktree* has always checked its own slug against `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,64}$` **before** the value reaches a shell command or a filesystem path — precisely so a path-traversal segment (`../`) or a shell metacharacter (`$(...)`, backticks) can never be interpolated into either. `agents/daedalus.md` derived `<source-slug>` from the resolved source and carried it into five places with no such check: `$BRIEF`, `${BRIEF%.md}.dispatches`, `${BRIEF%.md}.audit`, the write-lock holder's `SLUG` field, and the `rm -f` that *Run cleanup* runs over the first three. The value itself did not change in #219, but that PR grew the number of call sites reading it unvalidated from one to five, which is what moved this from a latent asymmetry to a gap worth closing. The guard is now mirrored **once, at the derivation site** rather than at each of the five uses — every later path is built from that single value, so one check at the source covers all of them, and the file says so explicitly to stop a future edit from scattering redundant copies. Its failure mode is the half a well-meaning implementation gets wrong: a slug that fails the check is a **hard stop** (`Blocked: invalid source-slug format`), never a sanitized fallback. Stripping the offending characters and continuing would point the run's scratch files — and the `rm -f` that later deletes them — at a path nobody chose, which is the exact deletion the guard exists to prevent. This is deliberately stricter than `athena`'s version, which degrades to reviewing in the shared tree: there the worktree is optional, whereas here the slug **is** the brief's path, so no reduced mode exists to fall back to. The stop also names itself the one terminating path that owes ***Run cleanup*** nothing — it fires before the brief, the dispatch ledger, and the audit trail exist, and the cleanup contract otherwise says those three are owed by every path without exception. Without that carve-out the guard reopens through its own cleanup door: honouring the contract literally means building `$BRIEF` from the rejected slug and running `rm -f` over it, which is the deletion the check just prevented. `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php` pins the pattern, the derivation-site placement, the hard stop, its Czech `Blocked` wording, and the cleanup carve-out next to the existing `athena` pin.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the Bash boundary hook no longer reads a heredoc body as command text (#222). `src/BashCommandTokenizer.php` knew no heredoc at all, so `<<` matched the redirection pattern as two separate `<` operators and the body was left to the ordinary scan. Any PHP object operator inside that body then registered as an output redirect, and the word after it became the redirect's target — so `AgentBashBoundaryGuard` denied the command as a write outside the agent's permitted paths. The block was a false positive twice over: a heredoc is an **input** redirection that writes nothing, and the body is a payload the command reads on stdin rather than text a shell ever executes. Its cost was concrete rather than theoretical. It stopped `athena` from publishing its review comment on PR #219, because the comment quoted Pest assertions from `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php`, and it reached every agent under the hook that publishes text containing a PHP arrow — in practice every code review of PHP. `BashCommandTokenizer` now recognises `<<WORD`, `<<'WORD'`, `<<"WORD"`, and `<<-WORD` (whitespace after the operator included), emits the opening as one operator word plus its delimiter, and skips the body as data from the next newline to the terminator line; `<<-` strips leading tabs so an indented terminator still closes the body. The opening and its body are separated by the rest of the command line, so a new `BashHeredoc` value object queues the opening until the scan reaches that newline — which is also what makes `cat <<A <<B` read A's body first, the order a shell uses. Two details keep the fix from trading one mis-parse for another. `<<<` is added to the redirection pattern and excluded from the heredoc pattern by a negative lookahead: a here-string takes its word on the same line and has no body, so reading it as a heredoc would swallow the rest of the command hunting for a terminator that never arrives. And an unterminated heredoc sets `ambiguous` instead of being assumed to run to the end of the command, which is the same fail-toward-`ask` stance the tokenizer already takes on an unterminated quote and a process substitution. Quoted content was never affected — `consumeStructure()` already runs before the redirection scan — but the new test pins the heredoc and the quoted surface together, so the invariant it states is the one that matters: payload text never moves the verdict. A second test holds the other direction, which is the one a fix like this can quietly break — a real redirection on the opening line, on either side of the opening, or after the terminator still reaches the write rule, and an unterminated body still resolves to `ask` rather than to the silent pass that skipping to the end of the command would produce.

- ✨ **Added**: the five skills that create or move a commit now apply the green-commit rule instead of only the git rules stating it (#233). A rule nothing applies is documentation, and the gap was concrete: `@skills/resolve-issue/SKILL.md` planned commits with no green requirement at all; its `references/phase-planning.md` told the author a bug commit carries *"the TDD failing test written first"* — a sentence that reads equally well as *commit the red test first* — and ordered commits so one may be *"a fix that must land before the test proving it"*, which the new rule forbids outright; `@skills/test-driven-development/SKILL.md` said nothing about commits, so nothing there stopped the RED state from becoming one; and `@skills/git-workflow/SKILL.md` plus `@skills/process-code-review/SKILL.md` both rebase a branch and push it with no replay in between. Each now carries the obligation it can actually enforce. `phase-planning.md` gains a *green on its own* bullet beside the existing *complete on its own* one, states that the failing test is written **in the working tree** and committed **with its fix**, drops the fix-before-its-test ordering example, and ends its reconciliation step with the replay. `test-driven-development` states that **RED is a state of the working tree, never a commit** — the cycle is unchanged, only the commit boundary is — and names the **Critical** severity a committed red state carries. `git-workflow`'s sync sequence gains `git rebase --exec` as step 3 of four, between the rebase and the force-push, with the reason spelled out: step 2 replayed every commit onto a different base, so each one now has a tree it was never gated against. `process-code-review` gains the same step on its own pull-policy path. `tests/Installer/GreenCommitRuleTest.php` derives both lists — cite-the-rule and replay-before-push — from an explicit roster of the five files, so a new history-authoring skill that skips the contract fails the build rather than shipping quietly.

- ✨ **Added**: every commit in a pull request must be green, not merely buildable (#233). `@rules/git/general.md` *Git Rules* already asked for cherry-pickable commits, but only that each one **"still build"** — a bar a commit clears while every test it ships fails, which is exactly the commit the next cherry-pick breaks on. That phrase now reads *"still pass the project's gate"*, and a new **Every commit is green** rule states the claim the old wording only implied: **every commit between the base and the branch head passes the project's own gate on its own**, never only the branch tip. Three obligations carry it. **A test and the change that makes it pass land in the same commit** — never a committed failing test, and never a test written to fail: no assertion of the buggy behaviour for a later commit to "fix", no `markTestIncomplete()` / `$this->fail()` placeholder standing in for the real assertion, no skipped test left to be enabled later. This does not weaken TDD: the RED step stays mandatory, stated here as a state of the **working tree** rather than a commit, which is the distinction that was missing and the reason a red commit could look compliant. **A history rewrite re-runs the gate** — a rebase, `--autosquash`, reorder, squash, or split changes the tree of every commit after the edit point, so a gate that passed before the rewrite proves nothing about the history that came out of it; the branch is replayed with `git rebase --exec '<gate>' <base>`, which stops on the first commit that fails, and a reshaped branch is never pushed unreplayed. **The gate is the project's own** — `composer build` / the Phing target / the CI workflow, never a hand-picked subset, because a commit that passes only the tests its author remembered is the one that breaks on the pick. The rule declares its own review severity so it cannot be waved through under the generic stratification: a committed failing or simulated-failing test is **Critical**, a branch rebased and pushed without the replay is **Moderate**. `tests/Installer/GreenCommitRuleTest.php` pins all three obligations, both severities, and the replaced *"still build"* wording.

- 💥 **Changed**: the implementer subagent is renamed `talos` → `hephaestus` (#231). Nothing about the role moved: same implementation run, same fast scoped-validation mode, same `Impl done` handoff, same `tools:` / `disallowedTools:` / `model: opus` frontmatter. Only the Greek figure changed, and it changed for a reason the naming convention itself states — an agent is named after the figure whose role matches its function, and `Talos` was the bronze automaton that *was* forged, while `Hephaestus` is the smith who forged him. The roster's builder is the smith, so the three etymology sentences (`agents/hephaestus.md`, `docs/agents.md` twice) are rewritten rather than search-replaced; a mechanical rename would have left the agent described as an automaton under a smith's name. The sweep follows the *Sweep the whole tree* rule the retirement section already carries: `agents/talos.md` → `agents/hephaestus.md` and its avatar with it, plus every reference in the other three agent files, `rules/`, `skills/`, `src/AgentBashBoundaryPolicy.php`, the content-pin tests, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `docs/`, and the `Related skill / agent` enumeration in the feature-request template. The moded dispatch-ledger roles are re-keyed with it (`hephaestus:impl`, `hephaestus:scoped`), as is the savings-mode coverage deferral (`deferred to hephaestus`). Two files are treated as history rather than swept: `CHANGELOG.md` keeps every past entry verbatim, and in `docs/memory/PROJECT_MEMORY.md` only the live `Trigger:` / `Rule:` / `Role:` fields are re-pointed while the dated `Example:` lines stay as written — the same split #96 applied when it retired `apollon`, and the reason is the same: a `Role:` field is a filter a specialist reads, an `Example:` line is a record of what happened. `assets/social-preview.svg` needed real work rather than a substitution, because `hephaestus` is four characters longer than `talos`: its chip widens from 130 px to 160 px and the whole row is re-laid out (`322` / `487` / `662` / `817`) so it stays centred on x=640 instead of drifting right, and `assets/social-preview.png` is re-rendered from the corrected vector with `rsvg-convert -w 1280 -h 640` so the digest pair in `tests/Installer/SocialPreviewAssetTest.php` binds raster to source again. `docs/agents.md` gains a **Renamed agents** section — deliberately separate from *Retired agents*, where every row means work moved to someone else — carrying the one consequence a rename shares with a retirement: the installer never deletes `.claude/agents/talos.md`, so an upgraded project keeps both names and can still dispatch the old one against a prompt nothing updates. **Run `vendor/bin/ai-olympus install --prune` after upgrading.** Not delivered: the issue also asks for a newly generated avatar in the style of the existing ones. `assets/agents/hephaestus.png` is the previous artwork carried over under the new name, so no roster surface renders a broken image, but the artwork still depicts Talos and needs regenerating by an image tool.

- 🗑️ **Removed**: the retired `metis` agent no longer appears anywhere in the package's live prose (#231). Its file and avatar were deleted back in #96, and `tests/Installer/AgentsTest.php` has asserted their absence ever since — but the name survived in three sentences that were never swept: `agents/athena.md`, `agents/daedalus.md`, and `@rules/code-review/general.mdc` each still offered *"a `metis` / `analyze-problem` plan artifact"* as a source for the remediation-conformance verdict. That is worse than a stale mention. An agent reading it is being told it may receive a plan from a subagent the roster cannot dispatch, so the sentence describes a routing option that cannot exist. All three now name `analyze-problem` alone, which is the skill that actually produces the artifact. The file-absence assertions were the wrong shape of guard — they proved the agent could not be *installed* while saying nothing about whether it was still *named* — so the same test now walks every text file the package ships and fails on any surviving occurrence, with two deliberate exemptions: `CHANGELOG.md`, which is the record of the removal, and the test itself, which must spell the name to forbid it.

- ✨ **Added**: code review now asks how a change behaves at volume, not only whether it is correct (#223). The gap was specific rather than general: `@rules/sql/optimalize.md` *Batch over per-row operations* and the matching CR walk already owned **DB round-trips issued per row inside a loop**, so a reviewer had a rule for `foreach { $model->update(…) }` and nothing at all for the two failures that survive perfect batching — **how much is held at once**, and **per-item work outside the database**. Both pass every test against a seeded fixture set and die against real volume, which is exactly why they survive review: the defect is invisible at the size the reviewer sees. `@rules/sql/optimalize.md` gains **Bounded reads over unbounded materialisation** — a set whose size grows with the business is read with `chunkById()` / `lazyById()` / `cursor()` and an explicit chunk size, never `Model::all()` or an unfiltered `->get()`; an `IN (…)` list built from an unbounded caller array is chunked before it reaches `max_allowed_packet` or the placeholder limit; and a set with a hard, small upper bound the schema or a `LIMIT` guarantees is materialised whole **without** a finding, so the rule does not flag every `->get()` in the codebase. Two of its points are load-bearing rather than stylistic: **`chunkById()` over `chunk()` when the loop writes to the set it reads** is a *correctness* argument — offset paging re-runs the query per page, so a row the loop moves out of the filtered set shifts every later page and the walk **silently skips rows**, processing a subset and reporting success — and **`cursor()` bounds PHP memory, not the driver's**, since the PDO connection still buffers the whole result unless it is in unbuffered mode, which is the trap in recommending `cursor()` as the general answer. `@rules/code-review/general.mdc` gains **Bulk Data & Batch Processing (issue #223)** plus its Core Analysis walk: three distinct defects, each with its own fix and its own severity — unbounded materialisation (**Moderate**, **Critical** when reachable from a request path with a caller-controlled filter, because the ceiling is then the attacker's to choose), offset paging while writing to the set being read (**Critical**, never downgraded — it is a correctness defect wearing a performance defect's clothes), and per-item work the platform can do in bulk (**Moderate**, **Critical** when each iteration crosses a network boundary carrying a rate limit or a per-call cost). The bulk primitives are **named** rather than implied — `Http::pool()`, `Notification::send($collection, …)`, `Bus::batch([...])`, `Cache::putMany()` / `Cache::many()` — so a Suggested Fix cannot degrade into "batch this". Two properties keep the addition from becoming noise. **Gating**: DB round-trips per row stay with *Per-row DB operations in loops*, a rewritten-and-slower query stays with *SQL query performance non-regression*, a value computed too early stays with *Variable Ordering & Lazy Evaluation*, and a line matching both that and this section is raised here once — never twice. And **every finding must state the volume at which the code fails** (*"one HTTP call per order; a 50 000-order export issues 50 000 calls against a 100/minute rate limit"*); a finding that cannot name the growth is not raised, because "this is inefficient" is an adjective a reviewer cannot argue with and an author cannot weigh. `skills/code-review/SKILL.md` enumerates its Core Analysis walk-through by name, so the new section is added to that list — a rule absent from it is a rule the review never reaches, and this is the only skill change the issue needs: every other CR and refactoring skill already applies these two rule files and defers to them. `tests/Installer/BulkDataReviewRuleTest.php` pins both rule sections, the named bulk primitives, the Critical severity on the skipped-rows defect, the gating clause, the volume requirement, and the skill enumeration.

- 💥 **Changed**: a job-dispatch assertion asserts the dispatch and nothing else (#181). `@rules/code-testing/general.mdc` *Jobs* gains the rule: `Queue::assertPushed()` takes the job class, never a closure. `Queue::assertPushed(ProcessPayment::class, fn (ProcessPayment $job): bool => $job->orderId === $order->id)` is a payload assertion wearing a dispatch assertion's clothes — it reads the job's own properties from the **caller's** test, so renaming a property or reshaping a constructor inside the job breaks a test that was never about the job's internals, and a predicate that matches nothing fails with *"the expected job was not pushed"*, pointing the reader at the dispatch that did happen instead of at the closure that did not match. A dispatch test owns exactly one fact — the caller dispatched the job — and what the job carries belongs in the job's own test, which constructs it with known inputs and asserts `handle()`. Two boundaries keep the rule from over-reaching. It covers the **sibling assertions that accept the same closure** (`Queue::assertNotPushed()`, `Queue::assertPushedOn()`, `Bus::assertDispatched()`, `Bus::assertNotDispatched()`, `Bus::assertDispatchedSync()`), because the rule is about *where* a payload is asserted and naming only `Queue::assertPushed` would leave it defeatable by spelling the assertion through another facade. And an **integer count** (`Queue::assertPushed(ProcessPayment::class, 2)`) stays allowed — it is still a fact about the dispatch, not about the job's contents. `@rules/code-review/general.mdc` gains the matching walk so the rule is enforced rather than merely written: it matches the six assertions, raises one **Moderate** finding per call whose last argument is a callable, and carries two literal **Suggested Fix** templates — *drop the closure* when the job's own test already covers the value, *move the payload check* when it does not — so removing a closure never silently drops the only coverage a property had, and `@skills/process-code-review/SKILL.md` can extract the fix deterministically. **No skill file changed**, deliberately: all six test-authoring and review skills already `Apply @rules/code-testing/general.mdc` and defer to it, and `docs/agents.md` forbids duplicating a rule into the skill that applies it. `tests/Installer/JobDispatchAssertionRuleTest.php` pins the prohibition, its reason, the place the payload belongs, every sibling assertion, the integer carve-out, the CR walk with its declared severity, and both fix templates — and walks every text file the package ships to prove no example contradicts the rule it just introduced.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the social-preview asset advertises the live agent roster again (#193). `assets/social-preview.svg` still carried a chip for **`argos`**, retired in #179, and had never gained **`hermes`** — the one surface #179 missed while it swept `README.md`, `docs/agents.md`, the rules, and every agent file in lockstep. #96 had kept the same asset in step when it removed `metis`, so the miss was an omission, not a policy. Two of the issue's own premises did not survive contact with the file and are corrected rather than implemented: the asset renders **four** chips, not five, and it never contained `apollon` at all — so the fix is a one-for-one substitution (`argos` → `hermes`) that leaves the live roster of `daedalus`, `talos`, `hermes`, `athena` exactly four names, not a five-slot layout to rebalance. `hermes` is six characters where `argos` was five, so its chip widens from 130 px to 140 px to match `athena`'s, and the row is re-centred on x=640 by shifting the first three chips 5 px left and the last 5 px right — the chip row stays symmetric instead of drifting right by the widened pill. `assets/social-preview.png` is re-rendered from the corrected vector with `rsvg-convert -w 1280 -h 640`, the size GitHub serves a social preview at. `tests/Installer/SocialPreviewAssetTest.php` closes the gap that let the drift live for two releases: the chip list is parsed out of the SVG and compared against `glob('agents/*.md')` — **derived, never pinned as a literal**, so the next roster change fails this test instead of leaving the asset stale again — and a second test binds the raster to its source through a digest pair plus a dimension check, because a PNG cannot be derived from its SVG at test time without a renderer in CI. Editing the vector alone now fails the build, which is precisely the failure mode this issue reports: the SVG was corrected once before and the PNG was never re-rendered.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the seven rules this package declared as always-on now actually reach a Claude Code session (#187). They lived in `.mdc` files — Cursor's extension — and Claude Code loads only `.md` from `.claude/rules/`, so `rules/api/general`, `compound-engineering/general`, `git/general`, `laravel/laravel`, `php/core-standards`, `sql/optimalize`, and `writing/general` were **silently inert** for every Claude Code user while their frontmatter kept promising `alwaysApply: true`. That is 186 KB of content the project believed was in force and that never reached the model at all — a correctness gap, not a context-size one, and the exact inverse of #162. The fix is the rename the maintainer chose, plus the frontmatter translation the rename forces: Claude Code has no `alwaysApply` or `globs` field, and per its memory documentation a rule **without** a `paths` key is loaded unconditionally while a `paths` list scopes it. Keeping the Cursor keys on a `.md` file would therefore have changed nothing they claim, so they are dropped and "always" is expressed as the absence of `paths` — the same key #188 established as the one Claude Code actually reads. **Scope is deliberately the seven files and no others.** The remaining eleven `.mdc` rules declare `alwaysApply: false`; the issue records them as *correctly* not force-loaded, so they stay `.mdc` and stay read-on-demand through the `@rules/…` references that already reach them. Renaming them too would have needed a `paths` value for the six that declare `globs: []` — and Claude Code documents only two states, absent (`unconditional`) and a list (`scoped`), with nothing for "never auto-load". Guessing at `paths: []` is the same category of mistake #188 corrected, and the alternative — dropping the key so a 133 KB code-review rule loads on every session — is worse than the defect. **The cost is stated rather than buried:** those seven files now load into every session, which is what `alwaysApply: true` always claimed and never delivered; whether that set should be smaller is #162's question, not this one's. 554 references across 91 tracked files were re-pointed at the new paths. `CHANGELOG.md` and `docs/memory/PROJECT_MEMORY.md` are deliberately **not** swept — they are append-only records of what happened, and rewriting a path inside a dated entry would falsify the record, so their `.mdc` links are stale by design and this entry is where a reader finds out why. README's rules table gains an accurate statement of what the two extensions mean, replacing the line that claimed all `.mdc` and `.md` files "are ready for automatic injection by Claude Code" — the claim this issue disproves. `tests/Installer/RuleExtensionLoadabilityTest.php` pins the always-on set as `.md`, pins that each one carries a `description` and no `paths` / `alwaysApply` / `globs`, and walks every tracked text file to prove no reference outside the two historical records still points at a retired `.mdc` path; the two existing tests that pinned `alwaysApply: true` are corrected to pin the key Claude Code reads, since pinning a Cursor-only key proved nothing about whether the rule loads. **Upgrading leaves a stale copy unless you prune.** The installer removes a file the source stopped shipping only under `--prune`, so a project that upgrades without it keeps both `.claude/rules/git/general.mdc` and the new `.claude/rules/git/general.md`. Claude Code reads only the `.md`, so the leftover is inert rather than conflicting — but it will drift, and Cursor would keep applying the stale copy. Run `vendor/bin/ai-olympus install --force --prune` after upgrading, the same remedy the retired-agent case already documents. **What this does not prove:** that a live Claude Code session now injects these seven files was not confirmed end-to-end in this non-interactive run — the documented `paths` semantics and the observed `.md`-only loading are the evidence, exactly as in #188.

- 🔒 **Security**: new opt-in installer flag `--enforce-agent-bash-boundary` registers a per-agent `PreToolUse` hook that hands every Bash call to this package's own validator (#185), closing the second of the two mechanisms #160 recorded as deferred. Off by default; without it no install writes a `hooks` entry of any kind and the Bash boundary stays exactly as advisory as before. With it, one entry with `matcher: "Bash"` is written to the project's `.claude/settings.local.json`, pointing at the binary's `bash-guard` subcommand with an explicit `timeout: 10`; Claude Code then asks that validator before every Bash call, and the validator answers from the payload's `agent_type` — so `athena` can be refused a `git commit` that `talos` is granted. **That per-agent scope is the whole point**: `permissions.allow` / `permissions.deny` are session-wide by construction, so `--deny-network-bash` (0.10.0) cannot express "this agent, not that one" no matter how it is written. Four decisions here are load-bearing rather than cosmetic. **The registration is a settings entry, not `hooks:` in `agents/*.md` frontmatter**, which would have been the more literal reading of "per agent": frontmatter hooks were reported not to execute for Task-launched subagents at all (anthropics/claude-code#18392, closed as a duplicate with no confirmed fix), they carry a workspace-trust gate of their own, and `agents/*.md` is distributed **unconditionally** by the installer — a `hooks:` line there would install a runtime component into every consuming project regardless of any flag, which is precisely what "the package grants no additional permissions by default" forbids. That field is now **banned** outright in agent frontmatter, alongside `memory:` and `permissionMode:` (#205), since the one issue that might have justified it decided against it. **The target file is project-local**, for the same reason as `--deny-network-bash`: hook and permission scopes merge, so a user-level entry would apply to every project on the machine and could not be relaxed by one that needs it off. **The validator never answers `allow`** — only `deny`, `ask`, or `defer` — because an `allow` would override the consuming project's own `permissions.deny`, including the entries `--deny-network-bash` had just written; the type it returns cannot express `allow` at all. **The binary is run once before anything is written** (`bash-guard --self-test`, which must answer `deny` and exit 0), because reading the settings file back proves only that the JSON landed, never that the command inside it executes — and since every hook failure is fail-open, an unrunnable binary would otherwise leave a reported protection and no actual one; a failure there fails the install loudly and writes no hook. **What this buys, stated precisely and not more:** each agent's own `## Bash boundary` becomes a question the harness asks a program, per agent, instead of an instruction only the model reads. **What it does not buy:** it is a check, not a sandbox, and every one of the following fails **open** — the command simply runs: an untrusted workspace (vendor-documented, and recorded as *not* empirically confirmed by this project rather than assumed), a session that started before the flag was installed (hooks are a session-start snapshot, which is why the summary line tells you to restart), an agent that deletes the entry or overwrites the validator (every agent has `Bash`; `talos` also has `Write`/`Edit`), a missing or slow binary and the 10-second timeout, `bypassPermissions` (**unverified** — it needs a live session to establish, and this project has not measured it), child processes of a permitted command, obfuscation (`c=curl; $c …`, `base64 -d | sh`, a script whose contents call `curl`), any tool other than `Bash`, and a payload that carries no `agent_type` (which degrades to the global rules and is never read as trustworthy). Two further limits were measured against Claude Code 2.1.221 rather than assumed: `defer` is honoured in print mode only — an interactive session ignores it with a warning, though the effect still matches the intent — and `claude --bare` / `--safe-mode` run no hooks at all. The OS-level tier that would cover child processes is Claude Code **sandboxing**, which this package still does not configure. `SECURITY.md` gains a `### --enforce-agent-bash-boundary` section with the entry shape, the scope, the eleven-item fail-open enumeration, the safety guarantees and a manual undo procedure (there is no inverse flag), and a row in the writes table; `docs/agents.md` gains a matching *Enforcing the per-agent Bash boundary* callout. The three places that would have become factually untrue on merge — `SECURITY.md` *Agent capability model & residual risk*, `docs/agents.md` *Why Bash stays advisory* and *Architecture constraint*, and `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *Bash capability boundary*, whose single opt-in exception becomes two — are rewritten so the honest framing survives the change instead of drifting into "the Bash boundary is enforced now": **it narrows the gap; it does not close it.** The **non-goal is unchanged and explicit**: the advisory `## Bash boundary` prose in `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` and in every `agents/*.md` stays the contract, and stays the fallback whenever this optional layer is not installed — which is the default.
- 💥 **Changed**: agents now file a tracker issue only for work that must actually be done (#225). `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *File deferred points as follow-up tracker issues* gains **The filing bar**, and the rule now separates two things it previously treated alike. A **promise the run made** — an assignment item the task required and the run did not deliver — is still filed unconditionally, because skipping it cuts scope in silence; the bar never weakens that. Everything an agent **notices on its own** — a *Refactoring Proposals* entry, an out-of-scope observation, an improvement idea — passes the bar first, and is filed only when it blocks or materially complicates a planned capability, is a Critical / High bug or a security shortcoming, or is technical debt with a **named, concrete consequence** (a limit already exceeded, an invariant that has already drifted apart in two places, a documented promise the code does not keep). Never filed: cosmetic or wording-only proposals, naming preferences, refactoring ideas with no named consequence (*"could be extracted"*), nice-to-have work, observations carrying no concrete step, and **mirror issues** — items already recorded in an existing issue, a rule, the changelog, or the reviewed PR body itself. The main producer of the noise was `agents/athena.md` step 9, which read *"Every out-of-scope item the review produced therefore also becomes an issue"* and filed the entire *Refactoring Proposals* section unconditionally; its heading, its opening claim, and its `What gets filed` bullet are rewritten around the bar, and the *Something real but out of scope* principle at the top of the file no longer promises unconditional filing either. `skills/resolve-issue/references/deferred-follow-up.md` gains a step 0 applying the same split. Two properties keep the change from losing information: **not filing is not dropping** — a withheld item stays in the published review's *Refactoring Proposals* section and the PR body, where the reader of that PR already sees it, and a real problem resurfaces on the next review of the same code — and a withheld item must be **named with its reason** in `athena`'s handoff under `withheld below the filing bar:`, so the judgement call stays visible to the human instead of vanishing into silence. The tie-break is stated explicitly: **when in doubt, do not file**, because an unfiled real problem comes back on its own while a filed non-problem is re-read, re-triaged, and re-prioritised by every later run and never leaves the backlog. Two tests in `tests/Installer/CompoundEngineeringContentTest.php` pin the three ways in, the never-file list, the unconditional-deferral carve-out, the handoff disclosure, and a negative assertion on the removed unconditional sentence. Per user request.

- ✨ **Added**: `writing/general.mdc` rule (#218) — every agent response and every published report follows the **ASD-STE100** simplified-technical-writing principles: one idea per sentence, roughly 20 words for an instruction, active voice with a named actor, present tense for what is true, one term per concept repeated rather than varied, numbered lists for sequences, and no marketing register or hedging where a fact is available. Two of the rules exist to stop the obvious over-correction: **no telegraphic compression** (simplifying removes ideas per sentence, never the articles and prepositions a sentence needs), and **short paragraphs** rather than one long block broken into fragments. The rule is `alwaysApply: true`, so it covers replies to the user, handoffs, PR descriptions, commit bodies, CR findings, and tracker comments alike. **It never forces English.** ASD-STE100 is written for English, but everything the rule mandates is a structural property of a sentence, so the principles apply in whatever language the output already is — a Czech report obeys the same discipline in Czech. The standard's **approved-word dictionary and English word forms are explicitly not imported**: an agent writing Czech does not restrict itself to an English vocabulary list and does not calque English syntax to imitate the standard. Code identifiers, paths, command names, and label strings stay verbatim in every language, because they are names a reader greps for, not prose. The relationship to the sibling language rule is stated in both files as a *Scope boundary*: `@rules/reports/general.mdc` decides **which** language a tracker report is written in, this rule decides **how** the sentences inside it are shaped, and a violation raises one finding under one rule — never two for the same line. `tests/Installer/WritingContentTest.php` pins each named rule, the language-neutrality section, the two-way cross-reference between the rules, and the README overview row, so the rule cannot be softened back into "write clearly". Per user request.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: `resolve-and-merge` now actually orders its batch by priority, instead of only appearing to (#216). The entry below describes ordering by a `priority:P0`…`priority:P3` label — but `select-candidates.sh` matched those with `^priority:p[0-3]$`, **no space after the colon**, and no repository has ever carried such a label: this one uses `priority: critical` / `high` / `medium` / `low`, the set `github-issue-triage` seeds and maintains, and #213 made that the taxonomy the whole package is triaged with. The two systems never overlapped by one character. The failure that produces is the quiet kind — the filter matches nothing, so **every** issue takes the "no priority label" fallback, every rank comes out equal, and `sort_by([.rank, .createdAt])` collapses into a pure oldest-first sort that still returns a well-formed, deterministic-looking batch. Nothing errors, nothing is reported, and each candidate carries `"priority": null` in a field whose whole purpose was to let the batch plan justify the order. Measured on this repository's own backlog before the fix, `select-candidates.sh 6 Resolve_by_AI` returned #103, #109, #181, #187, #193, #212 — all `null` — putting two July `priority: low` chores at the head of the queue and #187, the only `priority: critical` issue open, **fourth**; after it, the same command returns #187, #193, #181, #212, #103, #109. The scale of the regression is worth stating plainly: the batch is strictly sequential, so on a full run the critical issue waited behind two documentation chores, which is the exact scenario the random draw was removed to prevent. The fix ranks `critical` → `high` → `medium` → `low`, keeps oldest `createdAt` first within one level, and keeps first-match-wins for the triage mistake of two priority labels on one issue — every rule the entry below states, unchanged, just aimed at labels that exist. **The default for an unlabeled issue is now `priority: medium`**, carrying over the semantics the old P2 default had: reachable without ever outranking an explicit critical or high. `priority` in the output changes from `"P0"`..`"P3"` to `"critical"`..`"low"`, and the `priority:P0`…`P3` labels stop being recognised at all — an issue still carrying one is untriaged, not silently ranked as though the retired label still meant something. The match tolerates a missing space (`priority:critical` ranks too) and stays case-insensitive, because the cost of that leniency is one character and the cost of the strictness was this bug. Which is also why the script gains an embedded `--self-test`, registered in `composer.json`'s `shell-self-tests` so `composer build` runs it: reading the filter is precisely what missed the defect for a whole release, so the six cases **run the script** over fixture backlogs with `gh` stubbed — a backlog whose `createdAt` order is deliberately the reverse of its priority order, so an ordering that ignores the labels cannot accidentally agree with the expected result; `COUNT` slicing the ordered list rather than the raw one; a `priority:P0` issue resolving as untriaged; two priority labels keeping the first; the claim-label and `EXCLUDE` filters surviving; and the emitted object carrying exactly the five documented fields, never the internal `rank`. `skills/resolve-and-merge/SKILL.md` is corrected in all four places it stated the old taxonomy (the `description:` frontmatter, the step 3 selection paragraph, the step 5 queue order, the report table's Priority column). The entry below is left as written — it documents what shipped in 0.10.0, and the P0…P3 vocabulary is the accurate record of that.

- ✨ **Added**: `github-issue-triage` skill (#213) — the repository's priority and type label taxonomy, promoted from something a human maintained by hand into two executable `gh` scripts plus the rules that drive them. `scripts/seed-labels.sh` creates or updates the 4 priority labels (`priority: critical` `b60205`, `high` `d93f0b`, `medium` `fbca04`, `low` `0e8a16`) and the 10 type labels (`bug`, `enhancement`, `documentation`, `question`, `test`, `refactor`, `chore`, `security`, `marketing`, `plan`) with `gh label create --force`, so a re-run repairs a drifted color or description instead of failing, and it deletes nothing. `scripts/assign-priorities.sh` walks every **open** issue, derives the type from the conventional-commits title prefix (`fix(…)` → `bug`, `feat(…)` / `[Feature]` → `enhancement`, `test(…)`, `refactor(…)`, `chore(…)`, `docs(…)`, `marketing:`, `PLAN (…)`), falls back to the issue-form body when the title carries no prefix, and derives the priority from that type (bug → high, enhancement → medium, everything else → low). Three properties make it safe to run unattended, and each is the deliberate answer to a way an automatic labeller destroys information. **The conventional-commits scope is not the type** — `test(security): …` is a `test`, not a `security` issue — which matters because `security` is used here as a *topic* that accompanies a primary type, so reading the scope as the type would have escalated four already-correct low-priority issues. **`priority: critical` is never assigned and never downgraded**: "blocks the core product" and "confirmed security incident" are not derivable from a title, so the script leaves that judgement to a human and preserves an existing critical instead of overwriting it with the `high` its type would imply. **A label is removed only when an explicit title prefix proves it wrong** — a contradicting primary type, or a contradicting non-critical priority — and never on the body fallback, so a weaker signal can add a label but never take one away; `security` and `question` are topic labels and are never removed. An issue whose type is derivable from neither title nor body is **reported as skipped**, not guessed at. The body fallback matches the `### ` headings the GitHub issue forms generate, anchored to the start of a line, so prose that merely says *"Expected behavior: …"* inside a feature request does not turn it into a bug; a body carrying the headings of **both** forms is likewise reported as a conflict rather than resolved by whichever form happens to be tested first. Both scripts also **print the repository they resolved from the working directory before their first write** — `gh` picks that target implicitly, and `assign-priorities.sh` is the only script in the package that removes labels, so a transcript that does not name the target cannot be audited afterwards. Verified against this repository's live backlog: the derivation reproduced the human-assigned labels on all 24 classifiable open issues with zero changes, and correctly skipped the one free-form title whose `priority: critical` a human had set. `assign-priorities.sh --self-test` runs offline in `composer build`: the derivation table, plus an end-to-end pass that stubs `gh`, runs the script itself over a fixture backlog, and asserts every branch of the add / remove / keep decision — including the exact `gh issue edit` arguments — so the one code path that can take a label away is proven by execution rather than by reading the source. The skill also documents picking work with `gh issue list --label "priority: …"`, and why the system is label-based at all: the Projects v2 priority field needs a token carrying the `project` OAuth scope (`gh auth refresh -s project`), which is not available for this repository, while labels need no extra scope and are visible to the CLI, the web UI, and the API alike.

- 💥 **Changed**: `resolve-and-merge` selects its batch **highest-priority first** instead of at random. `select-candidates.sh` drops `sort -R` and orders eligible issues by their `priority:P0`…`priority:P3` label (P0 first), breaking ties on oldest `createdAt`; each returned candidate now carries its resolved `priority` field so the batch plan can state why a task holds its position rather than asserting an order the caller cannot verify. The random draw was not merely arbitrary, it was **actively harmful on a real backlog**: measured against a 56-issue queue holding both a P0 credential leak into a log file and a P3 documentation tidy-up, a uniform sample starts the tidy-up as readily as the leak — and since the batch is strictly sequential, the P0 then waits behind it for the whole run. Determinism is the second half of the fix: the same backlog now yields the same batch, which is what makes a task deferred in one run predictably reachable in the next, where a re-draw could keep passing it over indefinitely. **An issue carrying no priority label sorts as P2** — the declared default level — rather than last: sorting untriaged work to the back starves it permanently once a backlog is large enough to fill every slot, while sorting it to the front would let an untriaged issue outrank an explicit P0. Repositories that have adopted no priority labels at all are therefore unaffected in ordering *relative to each other* but do change behaviour in one way worth stating plainly: every issue ranks equal and the tie-break applies, so selection becomes **oldest-first and deterministic** where it used to be random. **Two priority labels on one issue** is treated as a triage mistake, not a case worth a tie-break rule — the first match wins, keeping the rank total and the run deterministic instead of failing a whole batch over one mislabeled issue. Queue-ordering rules 1 and 2 (adopted pull requests, blockers before dependents) deliberately **override** priority: an adopted PR is work already half-done, and no priority makes it possible to ship a dependent task before its blocker; a blocker substituted into the queue keeps **its own** priority, not the priority of the candidate it displaced. Verified against a real backlog and against a synthetic set covering the edges — a P0 outranks a six-year-older P3, a label differing only in case (`PRIORITY:p2`) resolves, an unlabeled issue lands between P2 and P3, and both the claim-label and `EXCLUDE` filters still hold. Per user request.

## [0.10.0] - 2026-08-07

- 🗑️ **Removed**: the `apollon` subagent is retired — the roster is now `daedalus`, `talos`, `athena`, `hermes`, and its two dispatched jobs were **split along the roster's existing capability line** rather than handed wholesale to one survivor. Nothing about the pipeline's shape changed: the same gates run in the same order, with the same handoff statuses (`Tests done (scoped)`, `Reporting done`); only the agent behind each is different. **Test authoring and the fast scoped validation gate went to `talos`** — test authoring was never really separable from the implementation run, since `@skills/resolve-issue/SKILL.md`, which `talos` already owns, drives the strict-TDD cycle and the changed-lines coverage gate itself, so the split meant one agent wrote the fix and a second was dispatched to cover code it had not written; the scoped gate became `talos`'s second mode, carried over behaviour-for-behaviour (same high-risk heuristic for scoped-vs-full build, same savings-mode build-gate cache). **Post-convergence reporting went to `hermes`**, not to `talos`: publishing to a tracker is messenger work, `hermes` already owned the canonical `upsert-comment.sh` wrapper and the publish-discipline that goes with it, and routing it there is what keeps the write-capable implementer from also acquiring the right to post under the user's identity — `talos` writes code and tests, and its verdicts travel in its handoff and the shared brief, never as a comment it posts itself. `hermes` stays on `model: haiku` because the reporting mode composes prose from evidence other agents already produced (the brief's `## Gathered context`, the converged PR, and `talos`'s scoped-validation handoff carrying the executed tests, coverage verdict, and acceptance-criteria statuses) — it runs no suite, authors no test, and derives no new fact. That also removes the old `light` / `full` reporting split: the "full" variant existed to re-run `create-test` / `e2e-testing` / `composer build`, and by the time reporting is dispatched the mandatory post-convergence scoped validation has already produced exactly that evidence. One consequence needed a real fix rather than a rename: `agents/daedalus.md`'s **dispatch ledger** keys an in-flight round on `{role, pr-head-sha, round}`, and with `talos` now dispatched up to three times per run the bare agent name stopped being a unique key — the second dispatch would have read as a repeat of the first and been suppressed by the in-flight check, so `<role>` now carries the mode (`talos:impl`, `talos:scoped`, `hermes:reporting`), pinned by a new test that also asserts the two steps which actually append a ledger line name the moded role they write. The savings-mode coverage deferral reads `deferred to talos` wherever it read `deferred to apollon` (`@rules/code-review/general.mdc` *Validation & Coverage Gate*, `@rules/code-testing/general.mdc`, `@rules/php/core-standards.mdc`, `skills/code-review-github/SKILL.md`), the `Role:` dictionary in `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` drops the value, and the consent inventory's two `apollon` rows collapse into one `hermes` row. `src/AgentBashBoundaryPolicy.php` loses its `apollon` rule set, which the parity test would otherwise have failed against a file that no longer exists. **`docs/agents.md` gains a *Retired agents* section** — a table mapping each retired name to what absorbed its work, plus the four rules a future retirement follows (name the successor **per responsibility, not per agent**, so a removal never widens one survivor's capability by accident; sweep the whole tree rather than `agents/` alone; re-key the dispatch ledger when a survivor gains a mode; add both the table row and a changelog entry). It also states the one thing a retirement cannot do on its own: the installer copies `agents/` but only *removes* a file the source stopped shipping when it is run with `--prune`, so a project that upgrades without that flag keeps a stale `.claude/agents/apollon.md` and the retired agent stays dispatchable there against a pipeline position nothing dispatches any more — **run `vendor/bin/ai-olympus install --prune` after upgrading**, or delete the file by hand. That section is the canonical answer for an agent or a human that finds a stale `@apollon` reference in an old PR, brief, or memory entry, which is why the dated `Example:` lines in `docs/memory/PROJECT_MEMORY.md` are left verbatim — they are the record of what happened, and rewriting history to hide a retired name would cost more than it buys — while every live `Trigger:` / `Rule:` / `Role:` field in that file is re-pointed. README's agent count drops from five to four. Per user request.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the *Clarifying questions* block a CR run puts on the JIRA comment no longer re-asks what the tracker already answered, and no longer carries questions whose answer changes nothing (#208). `skills/code-review-jira/SKILL.md` said only *"collect every genuine open question"*, which left two failure modes that appear at runtime rather than on the page. A CR run repeats on every iteration and each run posts a **fresh** JIRA comment, so a question asked in run 1 and answered in a comment was asked again in run 2 and in run 3 — the reader sees an unchanged block, concludes the reply went unread, and stops answering; the ask degrades exactly as the assignment gets clearer. And with no stated lower bound, a question about naming or wording — one that would change neither the implementation nor the accept / reject decision — sat on a ticket a non-developer reads next to the ones that genuinely block acceptance. Two gates now run before the block is assembled, in a fixed order so a Minor question is dropped rather than walked. The **severity gate** keeps only **Critical** (without the answer the change cannot be accepted at all — a missing acceptance criterion the implementation had to guess, a contradiction pointing at two incompatible behaviours, a blocking documentation request from the Third-Party API & Service Analysis step 7) and **Moderate** (the change ships either way, but the answer decides whether the behaviour it already implements is the intended one — an edge case resolved by picking a default, an unspecified limit, an unstated failure mode); Minor is dropped outright. That severity is a property of the **question**, not of any finding it relates to, and it is **never rendered** — the block stays plain sentences in Critical-before-Moderate order, because JIRA carries no severity vocabulary by the same constraint that keeps file paths and counts off that comment. The **already-answered walk** then re-reads every comment step 1 (`scripts/load-issue.sh`) has **already loaded** — the JIRA issue's comments and every subtask's, the linked GitHub PR description and its review comments, and the comments of each `closingIssues[]` entry — and issues no second fetch. A question is dropped only when **both** halves hold: a comment answers it **and** the reviewed diff actually implements that answer, with the second half verified **against the code, never against the comment's own claim** that the work was done. The two remaining cases are routed rather than guessed: answered-but-diverging stops being a question at all and becomes a finding on the GitHub PR comment against the answering comment as its source (Critical when the decision was an acceptance criterion, Moderate otherwise), so it lands where findings get fixed instead of where questions get asked; and an ambiguous reply — one that restates the question, defers it, or answers a neighbouring one — keeps the question, because asking twice costs a reader a moment while dropping a live blocker costs a wrong implementation. A dropped question leaves no trace on the comment: no "previously answered" note, no citation of the answering comment, since the chronological comment sequence already holds both the question and its answer. Scope is exactly one skill — `code-review-github` and `code-review-bugsnag` generate no *Clarifying questions* block at all, and `skills/pr-summary/SKILL.md` only appends the block verbatim, so neither needed a change. A new test in `tests/Installer/CodeReviewContentTest.php` pins both gates, their order, the "both halves" condition, and the against-the-code verification, so the rules cannot be reworded into advice.

- 🔒 **Security**: new opt-in installer flag `--deny-network-bash` writes session-wide `permissions.deny` entries for outbound-network Bash commands (#184), the first of the two mechanisms #160 recorded as deferred. Off by default; the package still grants and restricts nothing unless a human asks. Ten patterns are appended to `permissions.deny` in the project's `.claude/settings.local.json`: `Bash(curl:*)`, `Bash(wget:*)`, `Bash(nc:*)`, `Bash(ncat:*)`, `Bash(netcat:*)`, `Bash(telnet:*)`, `Bash(ssh:*)`, `Bash(scp:*)`, `Bash(sftp:*)`, `Bash(openssl s_client:*)`. Three of those choices are load-bearing rather than cosmetic. **The target file is project-local, not `~/.claude/settings.json`** — permission rules merge across scopes and are evaluated deny → ask → allow with specificity ignored, so a deny written into the user-level file would apply to every project on the machine and could not be relaxed by a project that genuinely needs `curl`; the only way back would be hand-editing the user's global settings. It also removes the `HOME` precondition that makes `--allow-bundled-scripts` a silent no-op — for a *security* control, silently reporting success without applying is worse than not shipping the flag, so the write is additionally read back and validated (`InstallerClaudeSettings::validateNetworkBashDenyPermissions()`) and the summary line prints only on a real write. **`openssl` is denied only through `s_client`**, because a deny rule cannot carry allow-list exceptions and a bare `Bash(openssl:*)` would permanently block `openssl dgst` — the checksum verification `@rules/security/backend.md` itself requires — along with `rand`, `x509`, `enc`. **`ncat` / `netcat` / `telnet` / `sftp` are listed separately** because the `:*` suffix enforces a word boundary: `Bash(nc:*)` does not match `ncat`. The `:*` form (rather than the documented-equivalent ` *`) is kept for consistency with `getBundledScriptPermissions()` and is now pinned by a test with the reason in a comment, so nobody "fixes" it. `InstallerPath::normalizeCliArguments()`'s hardcoded flag alternation gained the new flag too — without it the flag would never have reached `InstallOptions::fromArgv()` from a concatenated argv blob, which is the same silent-no-op failure mode in a different place, and a test now asserts the flag actually arrives rather than only that the blob splits. **What this buys, stated precisely and not more:** permission rules are enforced by Claude Code, not by the model, so those ten literal command strings move from an advisory instruction the harness never checked to a refusal the harness performs — real against prompt injection and drift. **What it does not buy:** it is not an egress control and not per-agent. A rule matches the command string Claude Code is asked to run, never the process tree it spawns, so child processes of allowed commands (`gh`, `git`, `composer`, `php -r`, `node -e`), unstripped wrappers (`bash -c 'curl …'`, `env`, `npx`, `docker exec`), absolute paths (`/usr/bin/curl`), the `/dev/tcp` shell built-in, unlisted tools (`socat`, `rsync`, …), and `--dangerously-skip-permissions` all remain open — which is also why this package's own `curl`/`ssh`-calling scripts (`skills/code-review-bugsnag/scripts/*`, `skills/_shared/attachments.sh`, `skills/_shared/assert-current-repo.sh`) keep working with every pattern in place. Being session-wide, it restricts the human's own interactive Bash in that project exactly as much as any agent's. The OS-level tier that would cover the first four bypasses is Claude Code **sandboxing**, which this package does not configure. `SECURITY.md` gains a `### --deny-network-bash` section carrying the pattern list, the scope, the full bypass enumeration and a manual undo procedure (there is no inverse flag), its *Agent capability model & residual risk* bullets are rewritten so the honest "advisory only" framing survives the change instead of drifting into "Bash network access is blocked", and the writes table gains a row; `docs/agents.md` *Why Bash stays advisory* and a new *Restricting outbound network Bash* callout say the same thing next to the capability model. **#185 (a per-agent `PreToolUse` validator) stays open** — this changes no agent's `tools:` / `disallowedTools:`, adds no `hooks:` frontmatter, and ships no runtime component.

- 🐛 **Fixed**: the audit-trail append obligation is now satisfiable by every agent it is assigned to (#194). `@rules/compound-engineering/general.mdc` *Audit trail for memory reads, outbound requests, and external writes* has obligated "every specialist" to append its own memory-read / outbound-request / external-write line to `.claude/run/<source-slug>.audit` since #167 — but `agents/talos.md`, `agents/apollon.md`, `agents/hermes.md`, and `agents/athena.md` never named `.audit` as a path their own `## Bash boundary` allow-list permitted writing to; only the shared brief was named there. The obligation was therefore structurally unsatisfiable for exactly the agents it named, confirmed empirically on the real `.claude/run/gh-183.audit` from the PR #192 run: it carried only `daedalus`'s own memory-read line, with no `external-write` line for that run's own PR creation. Of the two remedies the issue named — granting the permission to each specialist, or moving the write to `daedalus` alone — this fix takes the **first**: each of the four agents' own `## Bash boundary` now names `cat >>` to `.claude/run/<source-slug>.audit` (under that file's own per-run append lock, mirroring the shared-brief append lock already documented there) as a permitted target, and each agent's own body states the write obligation for the action classes it actually performs — `talos` (memory-read, `gh`/`acli` reads, opening the PR), `apollon` (memory-read, `gh` reads, and the reporting-mode publish only when it runs), `hermes` (memory-read, `gh` reads, an explicit publish), and `athena` (memory-read, `gh`/`WebFetch`/`WebSearch`, publishing the review and filing out-of-scope issues) — as a new step 12, deliberately kept distinct from her pre-existing step-11 **read** obligation so read and write are never conflated. The rule's own *Who appends* clause now cross-references each agent's Bash boundary, so a future obligation added to one without the matching permission in the other is a defect, not a silent gap. This does **not** change what the trail is: it stays self-reported and explicitly incomplete — a raw `curl` through `Bash` still produces no automatic line — and no existing L1/L2/L3 consent-level classification changes, since appending to `.audit` is bookkeeping alongside an already-classified action (PR creation was already L1 for `talos`, publishing was already L1/L2 for `athena`/`hermes`), never a new externally-visible action of its own. A new DERIVED test in `tests/Installer/CompoundEngineeringContentTest.php` globs `agents/*.md` and asserts every agent whose body claims the obligation also names the `.audit` append inside its own `## Bash boundary` section, so the two can never drift apart silently again.

- 🔒 **Security**: five residual capability-access gaps left after #186 are closed, verified against the actual state of `master` rather than against the changelog (#160). Each one is labelled by what it actually buys, since the architecture constraint (`docs/agents.md` *Architecture constraint*) still holds — this package ships instructions, never a permission engine. **Enforced (a test fails the build):** no `agents/*.md` frontmatter may declare `memory:` or `permissionMode:`. `memory:` automatically grants `Read`, `Write` **and** `Edit` to whichever agent declares it, so adding it to a read-only agent would have restored write access while passing every existing pin — those check the `tools:` and `disallowedTools:` lines byte-for-byte, and `memory:` touches neither. Vendor documentation confirms the implicit grant but does **not** document whether `disallowedTools:` strips it back off, so the ban rests on the new derived test over `glob(agents/*.md)`, never on that assumption; `hooks:` is deliberately left allowed, because #185 may introduce it as the per-agent enforcement of the Bash boundary. Two further tests derive their scope from the same glob rather than from a literal role list, so the next agent added to the roster fails the build instead of shipping the gap: every agent must appear in the consent-level inventory, and every agent's *Output / handoff* section must carry a `**Audit:**` item. **Obligation (an instruction agents follow; nothing intercepts a violation):** the per-dispatch memory slice is now authoritative **only** in its own structural position — its own top-level section of the `Task` dispatch prompt. The slice moved out of the shared brief into that prompt in #165, but no rule ever declared the prompt's quoted tracker text inert, while the same rule tells a specialist the slice is already filtered and that it must **not** re-read `docs/memory/PROJECT_MEMORY.md` — so a `## Project memory — <role>` section forged in a tracker comment (which needs no maintainer permission on an already-labelled issue) would have *displaced* the real memory rather than merely joined it, and a memory entry's `Rule:` field is a behavioural instruction. `daedalus` now fences every tracker quote it puts in a dispatch prompt as inert data, exactly as it already does for `## Gathered context`, and each specialist states that the same heading found anywhere else is quoted data. The audit trail gains a durable copy on the run shapes that previously left none: the rule named the PR body as the only permanent transcription, so an analysis-only run, an announce-only run, and a `Blocked` stop each deleted `.claude/run/<source-slug>.audit` at cleanup with nothing surviving — now `daedalus` renders the run total into its final report and every agent carries its own audit lines in its handoff. `agents/daedalus.md` *Audit trail ledger* also showed no line template while the adjacent *Dispatch ledger* did, and the nearest visible template was taken: a real run's `.audit` held `daedalus|-|gather|delivered|<ts>` — a dispatch-ledger transition line — and no `memory-read` line at all for the gather-phase read the brief itself documents; the shape is now restated inline, the wrong shape is forbidden explicitly, and the rule states that a line whose third field is not one of the three event classes is not an audit record at all. Finally the consent inventory, whose single deliverable was *one list to review a new externally-visible action against*, was already stale: `apollon` publishes the post-convergence feedback comment to the source tracker and commits authored tests, `talos` pushes the feature branch and writes the claim label, and `athena` promotes a converged PR out of Draft — none of them appeared in any row — so the five missing rows are added along with a *Keep this inventory complete* rule (add the row in the same change that introduces the action; default to **L2** when the level is uncertain). **Advisory, unchanged:** everything inside `Bash` remains a declared boundary the harness does not check, and the audit trail remains self-reported — a raw `curl` still produces no line. #184 (session-wide `permissions.deny`) and #185 (per-agent `PreToolUse` validator) stay open as separately tracked follow-ups; nothing here enforces the Bash boundary.

- ✨ **Added**: `skill-creator` now states the **directory layout** a new skill must follow, taken from Anthropic's own guidance rather than from repo habit ([skill authoring best practices](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/ai-olympus/best-practices), [Claude Code skills](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/skills), both fetched 2026-08-04). `SKILL.md` is the required entrypoint; `references/` holds material read on demand, `scripts/` utilities that are executed and never loaded into context, `templates/` the output shapes to fill in — the three folder names the repo's existing skills already use (6 / 4 / 12 occurrences at the time of writing), kept rather than renamed so the tree stays portable to another Agent Skills host. Five rules travel with the layout, each one load-bearing rather than decorative: keep the body under 500 lines and move detail into a sibling file (the same limit `skill-check.config.json` already enforces, now stated with the reason behind it); **keep every reference one level deep from `SKILL.md`**, because a file reachable only through another file gets previewed with `head -100` instead of read whole; say explicitly whether a script is **executed** ("Run `scripts/validate.sh`") or **read** ("see `scripts/validate.sh` for the algorithm"), since those are different instructions and execution is the cheaper, more reliable default; name files for their content and forward-slash every path; and open a reference file over 100 lines with a table of contents so a partial read still shows the full scope. Step 3 additionally gains the **portability rules of the Agent Skills spec** — the ones a skill can break while `skill-check` still passes, and then fail to load elsewhere: `name` is lowercase letters / numbers / hyphens only, carries no XML tags, never uses the reserved words `anthropic` or `claude`, and must match the directory the `/<slug>` invocation resolves from; `description` is non-empty, ≤ 1024 chars, XML-free, and written in the **third person**, because it is injected into the system prompt and is the only thing a host reads before deciding to load the skill. The repo's existing "Use when …" opening covers the *when* half of that requirement — the guidance now says the *what* has to sit in the same sentence rather than being left to the body. Per user request.

- ✨ **Added**: `resolve-and-merge` ships three **read-only** scripts under `scripts/`, so the deterministic parts of a batch that merges into the default branch are provably incapable of causing the damage the batch otherwise can: `preflight.sh` (session, repository slug, default branch, clean-tree check — exit `4` on a dirty tree, because an unrelated uncommitted change otherwise rides into the first task's commit and reaches the default branch under that task's name), `inventory-open-prs.sh` (classifies every open PR adopt / ignore from positive label evidence on the PR or on an issue it closes, resolving the labelled-issue set in **one** read instead of a request per PR), and `select-candidates.sh` (applies all three eligibility filters at once and draws at random). `_lib.sh` carries the shared contract: `gh` / `git` reads only — no tracker write, no merge, no working-tree change, no temporary file; every caller value reaches `jq` through `--arg` rather than string-concatenation into a filter, which is both the injection fix and what lets an arbitrary label (quotes, emoji) work at all; and every issue / PR title is stripped of C0/C1 control, bidi-override, and zero-width codepoints before printing, so an author-controlled title cannot rewrite or reverse the line a human reads before merging (the persisted-Trojan-Source shape, CVE-2021-42574). Two defects were found by running the scripts against this repository rather than by reading them: the sanitizer's first form used a `\uXXXX` regex character class, which jq's own string escaping consumes before the regex engine sees it — it silently stripped **every ASCII letter**, turning `feat(skills): add …` into `()  v--  --`, and is now codepoint filtering via `explode`/`implode` where each boundary means what it says and accented characters are never in range; and `sort -R | head -n` under `set -o pipefail` exits `141` via SIGPIPE precisely when there are more eligible issues than slots — the common case — so the slice moved into `jq '.[:$n]'`. Per user request.

- ✨ **Added**: `resolve-and-merge` skill — a batch entry point that takes several auto-resolvable GitHub issues from open to merged in one run, where `resolve-issue` handles a single known issue and stops at the pull request. Five behaviours the existing single-issue chain never had to decide are now stated explicitly, because each one is a place a batch run silently does the wrong thing: **open pull requests are inventoried before any new work is opened** — a PR is *adopted* into the batch only when it, or an issue in its `closingIssuesReferences`, carries the label, it re-enters the pipeline at the review step rather than at implementation (its branch and commits already exist), and every other open PR is *ignored* and left byte-for-byte untouched, never closed or commented on, with a PR whose linked issue cannot be determined ignored rather than adopted on a guess. **Selection is random, not oldest-first**, drawn with `sort -R` rather than `shuf`, which is not installed on macOS. **Dependencies are resolved before the queue is committed to**: a candidate blocked by another open issue is replaced by the *root* of its blocker chain when that blocker carries the label (the dependent is reported deferred, not resolved), dropped and reported when the blocker is unlabeled — never widened into working an unlabeled issue — and two candidates sharing one root blocker collapse into that single task. **The queue runs strictly sequentially on the shared working tree with no git worktree on the writing path**, mirroring `agents/daedalus.md` *Worktrees — writing path stays on the shared tree*; the read-only CR worktree stays `athena`'s own opt-in and is never requested here. **A blocker does not kill the batch** — a merge conflict, a failing check, an unconverged review, or a stalled dispatch is recorded verbatim and the run continues with the next task, since abandoning four remaining tasks because the first one failed is the failure mode a batch skill exists to avoid; the whole batch stops only when the repository state itself is unsafe (dirty shared tree, lost `gh` session, unmergeable base). The merge gate is unchanged and not re-implemented: `merge-github-pr` owns `isDraft` / `mergeable` / `mergeStateStatus` / `statusCheckRollup[]`, and the 0 Critical + 0 Moderate convergence requirement of `@rules/git/general.mdc` *Merging* stands — never force-merged, never relaxed to land a task. A watchdog nudges a silent orchestrator after 20 minutes of no observable progress (at most three nudges, then the task is recorded `stalled` and the queue moves on), and it states plainly what it cannot do: a blocking dispatch offers no mid-run channel, so the report says the watchdog could not fire rather than claiming a nudge was sent. This is the 52nd skill in the catalog; the two content-pin tests (`tests/Installer/SkillCatalogContentTest.php`, `tests/Installer/SkillsContentTest.php`) derive the count from the skills directory and pin `README.md`'s literals, so the README numbers cannot silently drift. Per user request.

- 💥 **Changed**: `ai-olympus install` no longer writes skills to `~/.claude/skills`; the home copy is now opt-in behind a new `--global` flag, and a new `--prune-global` removes the copies an earlier version left there. Skills always installed to **both** `.claude/skills` and `~/.claude/skills`, which is the wrong way round for how Claude Code resolves a name collision: per its documentation, *"enterprise overrides personal, and personal overrides project"* — so the home copy shadowed each project's own, in every project on the machine. Verified empirically before changing anything, not taken from the docs alone: two same-named skills with distinct markers, one per location, resolved to the home one (`Base directory: ~/.claude/skills/…`).
